Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Far better for the U.S. economy and for everyone who has a stake in it would have been a reckoning for Nvidia that forced it to undertake the hard work of corporate reform and rebuilding trust. Nvidia, for those who have not been paying attention, makes the most indispensable product in the world of AI: graphics processing units, a specialized type of chip, so capable and advanced that it has few real competitors, including, crucially, among well-funded Chinese companies. Nvidia, however, is much more than even this. As I have written before , it sits at the center of a highly incestuous economy of AI companies, including most of the big service providers, which it funds to keep purchasing its chips. The circular funding arrangements that Nvidia sustains with its largest customers—companies with extraordinary financial leverage and seemingly bottomless capital needs—is what keeps the AI economy’s party going. * Democrats Want to Make Life More Affordable. They Could Begin With Permitting Reform.
  Foreign Policy | 23 Nov 19:29 UTC
  The pros and cons of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. A bill that passed the Senate last week to reform the nation’s permitting laws offers Democrats—who voted two-to-one against the bill in the Senate—an early test of whether they can make daily life more affordable for Americans, even before taking control in January. Permitting reform may seem like a narrow or technical topic, but this is the most important piece of energy legislation in years. By speeding up environmental reviews, limiting litigation delays, and easing transmission siting, it would expand clean energy, lower energy prices, strengthen our energy system, and make future climate action more effective. The bill, known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, would make it easier to build transmission lines and other energy infrastructure, including both clean energy and oil and gas projects. That breadth has delighted advocates of abundance, the movement that argues that shortcomings in public infrastructure stem from regulatory bottlenecks, and enraged many environmentalists. Some supporters have been too quick to dismiss legitimate concerns, while some climate activists have drowned out substantive but highly technical legal objections with reflexive anti-fossil fuel rhetoric. As the bill heads to a House vote, a careful assessment of its provisions should lead members to support it while pursuing opportunities where possible to address credible objections in the final stages of the legislative process. The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. * Ukraine’s Energy Plan Was Too Little, Too Late
  Foreign Policy | 23 Nov 17:25 UTC
  The country’s push for renewables fell short—and now it’s paying the price.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Russian strikes are pounding the conventional energy infrastructure that Ukrainians depend upon to survive the winter, raising fears that some cities, among them the capital, Kyiv, could become uninhabitable. With exactly this horror scenario in mind, Ukraine raced this spring and summer to expand decentralized energy systems—mostly small gas-fired power plants and renewable energy systems—to replace its bulky, big-target coal-fired plants as a way of better ensuring energy supply for critical infrastructure. Government officials and most analysts in Ukraine concur that renewables and nuclear will underpin the country’s energy and national security, and that natural gas is required to steady the grid. But despite record rollouts of decentralized generation systems this year, the shift to a resilient energy grid is moving far more slowly than the winter and the war demand. Russian strikes are pounding the conventional energy infrastructure that Ukrainians depend upon to survive the winter, raising fears that some cities, among them the capital, Kyiv, could become uninhabitable. With exactly this horror scenario in mind, Ukraine raced this spring and summer to expand decentralized energy systems—mostly small gas-fired power plants and renewable energy systems—to replace its bulky, big-target coal-fired plants as a way of better ensuring energy supply for critical infrastructure. Government officials and most analysts in Ukraine concur that renewables and nuclear will underpin the country’s energy and national security, and that natural gas is required to steady the grid. But despite record rollouts of decentralized generation systems this year, the shift to a resilient energy grid is moving far more slowly than the winter and the war demand. Under unusually dire wartime conditions, Ukraine expanded its total renewable generation capacity by roughly 16 percent by mid-2026 compared to early 2025. The country added 70 wind turbines in the first half of the year, mostly in the Mykolaiv and Odesa regions, and is presently bringing online a wind power plant close to the Black Sea, the largest wind farm ever built in a country at war. Ukraine also added enough of solar generation to its supply this year to power roughly 600,000 to 800,000 European households, with European countries bankrolling rooftop solar-plus-battery systems for over 100 healthcare facilities. Ukraine’s battery storage rollout in 2025 outpaced every EU
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/23/ukraine-winter-energy-renewables-russian-strikes-infrastructure/

Ukrainian parliamentarian Oleksiy Goncharenko is deeply divisive in his country. In October, he was expelled from his parliamentary faction over a statement advocating for peace negotiations, in which he wrote that Ukraine “cannot defeat Russia.” He proposed several possible compromises with Russia that are viewed as politically toxic in Ukraine, including legal protections for the Russian language. Critics slammed the plan as being based on a false premise that Russia is interested in anything less than Ukraine’s surrender. Still, Goncharenko wields a measure of influence, thanks to his seat in parliament as well as the 1.43 million subscribers to his YouTube channel and his 200,000 subscribers on Telegram, the latter of which is particularly popular in Ukraine. Goncharenko’s political message may also be increasingly attractive to some Ukrainians as they face yet another hard winter under Russian attack, an increasingly unstable economy, and social tension over the military draft, a topic on which he is also vocal. And the success of a recent cease-fire in the Ukrainian town of Oleshky looks a lot like what Goncharenko proposed as a potential model for negotiations. Foreign Policy spoke to Goncharenko for a sit-down interview in Kyiv on Nov. 10—right as U.S.-mediated talks between Russia and Ukraine were taking place in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. * COP31: Key outcomes agreed at the UN climate talks in Antalya
  Carbon Brief | 23 Nov 16:15 UTC
  The COP31 climate talks in Antalya, Turkey, have failed to reach agreement on a “pact” that lacked clear references to… The post COP31: Key outcomes agreed at the UN climate talks in Antalya appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The COP31 climate talks in Antalya, Turkey, have failed to reach agreement on a “pact” that lacked clear references to curbing fossil fuels or scaling up climate finance . The “Antalya pact”, proposed by the joint Turkish-Australian presidency, was rejected in the final hours by the EU, the UK and several climate-vulnerable nations. Meanwhile, many developing countries criticised the lack of progress on climate finance and indecision on a proposed $120bn target for funding climate adaptation. With stark divisions preventing agreement on the presidency-led pact, UNFCCC chief Simon Stiell expressed “deep disappointment”, speaking of “scope to improve, modernise and reform” the UN climate process. Despite a chaotic endgame that saw the overrunning COP become the longest in history, it did not end in complete failure, with many items adopted in the closing plenary. These included fairly limited or procedural outcomes on a new “ mechanism ” for global “ just transitions ”, plus support for countries struck by climate disasters. There was some progress beyond the negotiations, though, with growing coalitions of nations championing “ roadmaps ” for “transitioning away” from fossil fuels. With the presidency promoting “ electrification ” as a key theme, around 80 nations also signed up to a global target of securing 35% of final energy consumption from electricity by 2035. Here, Carbon Brief provides in-depth analysis of all the key outcomes from Antalya, both inside and outside the COP31 negotiations. (See Carbon Brief’s coverage of COP30 , COP29 , COP28 , COP27 , COP26 , COP25 , COP24 , COP23 , COP22 , COP21 and COP20 .) Turkey and Australia presided over the longest COP in history – and failed to find consensus on their proposed “cover decision”, exposing deep fractures between countries on key issues such as fossil fuels and the need for developed nations to meet climate-finance pledges. Experts told Carbon Brief that, strictly, the talks did not “collapse” as several negotiated items were still adopted. Rather, the rejection of the cover decision represented, for the first time, “those who want a higher level of ambition actively reject[ing] the lowest common denominator on offer”, said one veteran observer. Dispute over who would host COP31 – representing the “western Europe and other states” region – had gone on for three years . In the end, the two main rivals, Turkey and Australia, agreed to a deal last year at COP30 that the summit would take place in An
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/cop31-key-outcomes-agreed-at-the-un-climate-talks-in-antalya

* Bond, Brexit and the Making of Modern Britain
  New Lines | 23 Nov 11:47 UTC
  Simon Winder joins Faisal Al Yafai to discuss Robert Pattinson’s casting as James Bond, how 007 consoled a shrinking Britain, and his thoughts on where — and when — the franchise should go next. The post Bond, Brexit and the Making of Modern Britain appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Hosted by Faisal Al Yafai Featuring Simon Winder Produced by Finbar Anderson Listen to and follow The Lede Apple Podcasts | Spotify | Youtube | Podbean We’ve been thinking about James Bond all wrong, Simon Winder tells Faisal Al Yafai on this week’s episode of The Lede. While the fictional British spy may have built an aura of invincibility in his seven decades at the forefront of pop culture, “James Bond is an ingenious folkloric figure of weakness,” says Winder, author of “The Man Who Saved Britain” and the forthcoming “Anglia: A Personal History of England.” Bond, Winder argues, offered a balm to British audiences in the mid-20th-century during a period of declining global relevance. “As Britain gets less and less important in the world, James Bond remains a kind of constant,” he says. “Fleming’s books and then the movies usher Britain from being a global power to being a bit of Europe, while reassuring them the whole time — millions of people reading the books, watching the films — that somehow, secretly at least, we’re still in charge.” That decline is particularly noticeable with regard to the United States, says Winder. “ Britain and America go through a period of being, like, broadly equals, and then Britain during the 20th century burns out,” he says. That dynamic is reflected in the relationship between the nations’ two intelligence agencies. “ The CIA is a colossal organization, whereas Bond, however ingenious he might be, is just a bloke with some sexual troubles and a gun,” Winder says. In many ways, Bond became the antithesis of the real politicians who represented Britain on the world stage. In the Suez Crisis, for example, “you can see what Anthony Eden is: ill, old, bust up, nostalgic for a previous period, wishes people in the Middle East just did as they were told,” Winder notes. “And there’s Nasser, who represents a particular form of virile, tough, non-class-based authority. … What do you do in the face of such a wave of anti-colonial energy, which Nasser fronted?” For Winder, Britain’s declining influence has meant the franchise has lost much of its relevance. “The fact he’s British, I think, with every passing year gets more and more stupid, because there’s quite a lot of countries where he’d be lucky to get a visa. Britain’s place in the world is really very local,” he says. Nonetheless, Winder approves of the announcement of Robert Pattinson as the seventh Bond in the franchise’s history. Pattinson, he says, is a “fantastically goo
  https://newlinesmag.com/podcast/bond-brexit-and-the-making-of-modern-britain/

We’re seeing the first generation in history for whom the line between humans and their representations could all but vanish. In this video essay for the digital publication Fully Human, the UK animator and filmmaker Lewis Heriz interrogates where this is taking us. Instead of submitting to a world where data and people are indistinguishable, we should be thinking critically and imaginatively about how we might use AI in ways that preserve our humanity. * The catch in IBM’s offer to relocate its U.S.-based immigrant workers
  Rest of World | 23 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Ending U.S. visa sponsorship and shifting roles abroad helps the company retain talent, but leaves employees paying a steep financial and personal price.
  FULL TEXT: For some IBM employees, keeping their jobs could mean moving to another country — and taking a steep pay cut in the process. Earlier this month, IBM said it would no longer support the U.S. immigration status of workers in its consulting division, offering them the option to take a role with the company in another country or exit, according to a Bloomberg report citing a company document. “IBM Consulting participates in U.S. high-skilled visa programs to address skill shortages,” an IBM spokesperson told Rest of World . “That participation has been declining for a number of years, and as skill availability and client need have evolved, we have opted to discontinue visa and green card sponsorship for the majority of such roles in IBM Consulting.” IBM Consulting employs more than 150,000 people globally. The company does not disclose the geographical split for its workforce, making it hard to ascertain the exact number of workers impacted by this decision. On November 19, the company’s career portal listed 1,278 open roles for the consulting division outside the U.S., including 1,125 in India, 23 in Singapore, and 18 in the U.K. In the U.S., it listed 89 roles. For employees, relocation does not automatically ensure continued engagement, effective collaboration, or equal access to professional opportunities, Zakaria Laaraj, founder of consultancy Global New Ventures, told Rest of World . It may affect their access to decision-makers, established networks, and career opportunities. “The credibility of such an offer depends on employee tenure, available positions, immigration requirements, and compensation,” Laaraj said. Discussions on Blind, an anonymous employee forum used by 14 million professionals, suggest relocating may not be realistic for many. Moving to India, for instance, could mean an approximately 80% pay cut, one IBM employee wrote on Blind. “Employees get three weeks to decide,” Blind’s CEO Sunguk Moon told Rest of World . “The alternative is three months of severance and a last day of December 31. And the news came in a surprise one-on-one ‘career sync’ with a manager.” For any relocation option to be viable, companies must go beyond just assigning a new job to their employee, George Carrillo, CEO of career development and staffing company HireMe, told Rest of World .   Aeon | 23 Nov 11:00 UTC
  El Niño has upended global weather, shaping the fate of societies, for millennia. Is this past a clue to a turbulent future? - by Dagomar Degroot Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Massive clouds of dust roll into Melbourne on 8 February 1983 as a consequence of droughts induced by the extreme El Niño of 1982-83. Photo by Trevor Farrar/Australian Bureau of Meteorology As I write these words, surface water temperatures across a great swath of the central and eastern Pacific Ocean have been rising for months. El Niño is the name for the recurring and sustained warming of this part of the Pacific. And temperatures are soaring to such highs, relative to their long-term averages, that the El Niño of 2026 may be the strongest in 1,000 years. It’s still uncertain how the warming ocean will reshape global weather in the months to come, let alone how ecosystems – and human populations – will respond. The future is always murky. Still, when it comes to El Niño, the past could teach us what to expect. And not just the recent past, but also the deep past recently recovered by scientists, historians and archaeologists. Global sea-surface temperature anomalies in November 2015, at the peak of the 2014-16 El Niño event, one of the strongest recorded by meteorological instruments. Courtesy NOAA I’m an environmental historian. I work with climatologists to understand how climates have changed in the past. I study how communities understood and adapted to the climate’s changes. And my research suggests that history can help us understand even the most extreme events in today’s rapidly warming climate. The past can help us see why El Niño is so hard to forecast, for example, or even define. It can show us that, while El Niño has contributed to the deaths of millions of people in the past, it has also enabled others to amass wealth and power. El Niño indisputably scrambles worldwide weather patterns, but history teaches us that it’s as much a human creation as a natural reality. And this context may help us weather not only the effects of the 2026-27 El Niño in the months to come, but also El Niño ’s future on a warming planet. A vast current of cold water snakes up the western coast of South America. In December each year, that current breaks down for about a month. An influx of warm water delivers tropical fish and – because warm water evaporates more readily than cold water – abundant rainfall. It’s impossible to know when people first noticed this annual reversal of water temperatures and precipitation. We do know that, sometime after the 16th-century Spanish conquest of the Inka Empire, fishermen named it El Niño , meaning literally ‘the boy’ or, w
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-can-we-learn-from-the-past-experience-of-el-nino

* The Leftist Group Raising Money for African Dictatorships
  New Lines | 23 Nov 10:39 UTC
  Why would the People’s Forum, which calls itself a “movement incubator for working class and marginalized communities,” hold a fundraiser for military dictatorships in Africa? The post The Leftist Group Raising Money for African Dictatorships appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On Oct. 17, a New York-based organization that calls itself a “movement incubator for working class and marginalized communities” held a fundraiser for three military dictatorships. The People’s Forum sold tickets to something they called the “Sahel Solidarity Fest,” telling buyers the proceeds would reach the “mass organizations and state structures” of Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger. Those three countries comprise the Alliance of Sahel States (the French acronym is AES), established in 2023 as an alternative to the Western-backed Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS). Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International and other humanitarian groups have documented forced disappearances, torture, conflict-related sexual assault, massacres of hundreds of people and ethnic cleansing in the AES dictatorships. All three countries have dissolved all political parties. The People’s Forum on the west side of Manhattan offered food, vendors and performances for the event, sponsored by a group called the Coalition for the Elimination of Imperialism in Africa and projected to run for four hours. The flyer had promised “addresses from all three AES permanent representatives to the UN,” naming Mali’s Ambassador Issa Konfourou, Burkina Faso’s Ambassador Saïdou Zongo and Niger’s Charge d’Affaires Samadou Ousman. Event organizers described the gathering as “a fundraising and solidarity event,” and its promotional material expanded on what that meant. “Here, we celebrate with solidarity and solidarity means concrete support to the forces on the frontlines,” declared the self-styled Marxist center. According to an attendee who spoke with New Lines , one speaker at the event told the audience that the funds would go to the “army of Mali.” New Lines was unable to independently confirm the remark, and the People’s Forum did not respond to a request for comment on the event’s beneficiaries. In April 2026, Human Rights Watch published a 316-page report on one of those AES front lines. It found that Burkinabe government forces and their allied militias killed, disappeared and looted their way through villages belonging to the Fulani ethnic minority until entire communities fled — conduct it said amounts to ethnic cleansing and could constitute a crime against humanity. Congress has barred U.S. assistance to the Malian government since 2020, to Burkina Faso’s since 2022 and to Niger’s since 2023.   https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/the-leftist-group-raising-money-for-african-dictatorships/

* The Old and The Furious
  Longreads | 20 Nov 19:12 UTC
  "As Americans live longer than ever, car crashes involving elderly adults are on the rise. The question is what to do about it—and how many more deadly accidents it will take for us to act."
  FULL TEXT: Americans are living longer these days, long enough to become vehicular terrors behind the wheel in their eighties and nineties, and they’re injuring themselves and others at unprecedented rates. David Howard, with additional reporting from Lloyd Alter, does the math and suggests that simply taking a senior’s licence away is not the answer; America is car-centric and many seniors need to drive in order to grocery shop, attend health appointments, and to meet their social needs. Could autonomous vehicles provide the balance we need between safety and independence? Already visible on the technological horizon, of course, are self-driving cars, which carry the potential to obviate many of the limitations older drivers face. Mark Needham (not his real name) lives on the fringes of Charlottesville, Virgina. He is 82 and still active: He practices yoga at a local health club, engages in a glassblowing hobby, and dabbles with technology. He and his wife, 80, have lived in the same subdivision for 40 years, and while she’s wrestling with several health issues and is ready to move into a retirement facility, he’s adamantly opposed to it. “My wife is more inclined to being taken care of,” Needham says. “I’m more inclined to be doing stuff.” Part of what allows him to maintain his independence: a Tesla that over his two-plus years of ownership has morphed, via a steady stream of software updates, into a largely autonomous vehicle. (Many reviewers and experts still cite the need for improvement with tasks like changing speeds and parking before use becomes widespread.) “It drives better than I drove 30 or 40 years ago,” he says. “Seniors are spending more time than ever on their slop-filled feeds. It’s their adult children’s job to save them.” “America’s elders need more places to live, but even in liberal enclaves that recognize the problem, developers are facing the fiercest foes of all: America’s elders.” “Devices that monitor seniors for safety are appealing to worried loved ones and underresourced home care agencies.”

  FULL TEXT: You have, by now, read something about the Enhanced Games, the steroid-fueled athletic spectacle held this year in Las Vegas. ( Perhaps we even recommended it .) But there’s nothing quite like seeing the event through Kent Russell’s eyes as he sneaks high-proof whiskey from his ThermoFlask, considers the physical demands of parenting and his own life’s onrushing decline, and concedes that a drug-enhanced life sounds “pretty fucking dope.” As of this moment, the sprinters are clean, the Erth bleachers are searing the backs of my thighs, and Michael Bryan is overawing me through the act of locomotion. I sip from my thermos and bask in wonderment that a human body can be made to move that way. Beauty of this kind, which eighteenth-century types correctly filed under the category of the sublime, requires that it exceed me. A thing I could do myself is admirable; a thing that no effort of mine could ever accomplish, that lives permanently past my ceiling, is awesome in the old, strong sense of the word, in which awe is part fear, part love, part recognition that I am small and soft. The unfairness is the source of the wonder. To resent that Bryan is gifted beyond me is to resent that the thing I’m watching is beautiful, which is insane and would require that I demand the world contain nothing larger than myself. “When I found the mushroom Amanita muscaria being sold in candy wrappers, I uncovered legal loopholes, dangerous dosages and more.” “How Jos Leijdekkers turned Sierra Leone into a drug-dealer’s paradise.” “It’s a new era of D.I.Y. medicine. * In Serbia, a Generation That Burned the Parliament Is Watching Its Successors Clean the Streets
  New Lines | 20 Nov 11:14 UTC
  We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship. The contrast is great, and I wonder: Did these students understand something that my generation did not? The post In Serbia, a Generation Th...
  FULL TEXT: I was sitting in the studio of an independent radio station that no longer exists today, when the voice of my colleague came on air. He was reporting from the streets of Belgrade. The city was full of people, tens of thousands of them, from all over the country, marching toward the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia. As he moved through the crowd, describing what was happening, one or more shots were heard in the distance. I remember his scared but also excited voice. He knew, just like me and many others, that we were witnessing the end of a regime. We were young, angry and determined to overthrow the government of Slobodan Milošević, which had brought us war, poverty, repression and international isolation. On that day, Oct. 5, 2000, we set fire to the assembly. Thanks to the refusal to obey the army and the police, a civil war did not occur, but it could have. The president of the country was arrested shortly after and taken to the international prison in Scheveningen, The Hague, and we firmly believed that we had won freedom, although we had no idea then how difficult it would be to build a democratic society and state. Twenty-six years later, a new generation of students won the elections and defeated the political establishment of Aleksandar Vučić, who had been in power for over a decade. On Oct. 25, 2026, after almost two years of protests, the student movement won the majority of votes in democratic elections and 140 seats in the National Assembly. After celebrations in the streets and squares of the capital, the citizens cleaned up the trash after themselves, and some of them took brushes and lime in their hands and started to paint over the graffiti of hatred and nationalism that proliferated on the facades of buildings and institutions over the past decade. We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship. The contrast is great, and I wonder: Did these students understand something that my generation did not? What I know is that the burning parliament did not bring us a better future, and what I want to believe, but don’t know for sure, is that the “cleaned city” is the symbolic beginning of creating a better society and a democratic state. I was 11 years old when I first saw, on television, protesters being beaten by the police on the streets of Belgrade. They fought against the regime of

* My grandmother was a seal
  Aeon | 20 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Many Irish families believed they descended from selkies. Their stories reveal the violence and grief in our kinship with nature - by Jordan Lee Borgman Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In October 1948, in Country Kerry, Ireland, Seán Ó Muircheartaigh met with Seosamh Ó Dálaigh to tell him about the woman who came from the sea. She had appeared, said Ó Muircheartaigh, one night on the magharees, those bright spits of sand that occasionally pop up amid the rain-lashed rocks of the Dingle peninsula. Alone on the beach, she settled herself on a rock and began combing her hair. Also on the beach was a farmer, who – much to the surprise of the woman herself – would by daybreak become her husband. He’d come to gather seaweed, seeing her before she saw him. Beautiful as she was, the woman was also strange: the creature he saw stagger onto that Dingle beach was not a woman at all. It was a seal. By the time Ó Muircheartaigh told Ó Dálaigh about the seal woman, he was already 80. He had been born in the apocalyptic aftermath of the Great Hunger that divested as much as a third of Ireland’s population to death or migration, and he had survived two great wars. Most of the disappeared were – like Ó Muircheartaigh himself – small farmers and native Irish, or Gaeilge, speakers. The famine had been not just a physical genocide: it had been a linguistic and cultural one, too. Both Ó Muircheartaigh and Ó Dálaigh were veteran contributors to the Irish Folklore Commission: the former as a renowned storyteller; the latter, an equally renowned collector. The IFC is now the National Folklore Collection at University College Dublin ­– a behemoth of 3 million pages of texts and recordings. But in 1948, following eight centuries of colonialism, the loss of 99 per cent of the island’s forests, a staggering emigration , and the decline of the Irish language, the IFC was in a race against time and the fragility of memory. As it turned out, an awful lot of people had heard stories about seals that came to land as humans. N owadays, these shapeshifters are known as ‘selkies’. Selkie is a Scots term that came into widespread use via a Joan Baez cover, ‘Silkie’ (1961), of a traditional Scottish folk song. Yet the phenomenon it describes – of creatures who shapeshift between human and seal forms by shedding a skin – appears consistently in folklore spanning the Celtic world and beyond, even as far as Sweden. Before Baez insinuated the term into international parlance, selkies were identified in Irish and English by a constellation of descriptors. In Irish, the most common is mhaighdean mhara , or ‘sea maiden’, referring to selkies and mermaids. Even in English, many stor
  https://aeon.co/essays/do-you-have-a-selkie-or-a-seal-in-the-family

* Infantino’s Path to a Fourth FIFA Presidency Runs Through Asia
  New Lines | 19 Nov 19:16 UTC
  The soccer chief’s ties to Trump have angered Europe and North America. He still looks set to win reelection, though, with Africa and Asia — led by Saudi Arabia and Qatar — backing him. The post Infantino’s Path to a Fourth FIFA Presidency Runs Through Asia appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Yousef Masoud/Anadolu via Getty Images) FIFA President Gianni Infantino was probably feeling confident and content on the morning of Nov. 19, even though the organization’s media department had just confirmed that the 56-year-old soccer chief would face two opponents in his reelection bid next March. One of those is Philippe Diallo, the French Football Federation boss. He was named just before the Nov. 18 deadline, a few days after Japan’s Kozo Tashima threw his hat in the ring. Dissatisfaction with Infantino is partly due to his closeness to U.S. President Donald Trump, symbolized by the creation of a FIFA Peace Prize that was handed to the American leader last December during the draw for the 2026 World Cup, co-hosted by the U.S. There was outrage during the tournament when FIFA intervened to overturn a red card given to the U.S. team’s star player, Folarin Balogun, so he could play in the round-of-16 clash with Belgium. Trump revealed that he had called Infantino to make it happen. In July, Infantino’s plan to sell off part of the World Cup to private investors was leaked. The backlash was fierce, despite those plans soon being scrapped. Three soccer confederations — representing Asia, Europe, and North and Central America and the Caribbean — signed an open letter calling for change. It looked as if Infantino’s days were numbered, but nothing happened. Meanwhile, the president took to the campaign trail. In October, his smiling face appeared with various presidents — of both associations and governments — in press release after press release and in Asian capital after Asian capital. Asia is a crucial battleground. If the confederations representing Europe and the Americas were against Infantino (it’s always hard to say if their members are completely united), and if Africa and South America were in favor, then Asia was split. Infantino was so successful on his travels that, on Oct. 23, the Asian Football Confederation’s leadership, recognizing that he had the backing of most of its members, performed a public U-turn and pledged its support. “You’ve visited a lot of countries in Asia,” the confederation’s president, Sheikh Salman bin Ibrahim Al Khalifa, said to Infantino. “I’ve spoken to a lot of members within this continent.   FULL TEXT: In this blunt, beautiful, and harrowing essay from Off Assignment , Blossom Gordana Champlain recounts learning of a mass, a foreign body next to her lung after visiting a German hospital. Raumforderung is a medical term arriving in my mind carrying more affect than information. I know that something improper and unwilling is happening. Instead of the valiant ingestion and revolutionary expulsion of the awful outside, instead of room for the paradox by which we all survive, there is crowding and concession. Displaced genes commanded wrongful growth, demanded space which could not be defended, and so was conceded, to a slow invisible thing, the bad truth I could ever only have failed to escape. “My neurologist says anything can be a headache. Welcome to the world of “First Amendment auditors,” a community of . . . well, let’s call them “enthusiastic enforcers of their own conception of what’s allowable on public property.” Get ready for your closeup! The auditors’ devotion to the U.S. Constitution and suspicion of authority might read to observers as libertarian-coded, but the YouTubers I spoke with claim not to have any partisan motivations. Rather, they’re asserting their right to record as a contrived stress test of whether public officials respect that right. Auditors’ MOs run the gamut. Some clearly seem to be goading police officers, government workers, and passersby into engaging with them. Others take a more stoic tack, refusing to answer questions or saying as little as possible. Still others identify as citizen journalists. My body felt good, better than I’d expected, better than I’d felt at home, where I spend too much time sitting at a desk. My body likes to move, apparently, to climb and descend; it’s fine with sleeping on the ground. My tricky left knee hurt a little bit, but it has since I was a teenager. I was tired at the end of each day, but hiking all day is tiring. I started to wonder why I had been so quick to decide I was, in the parlance of my parents’ generation, over the hill. Why had I insisted on mourning something I hadn’t actually lost? “He planned a multiyear trek through rivers, forests and snow in the Canadian wilderness. When Maureen Paschal, a college counselor in North Carolina, sent her daughter off to college a few years ago, she was promptly invited to join a group chat for moms of the girls living on the same floor. The moms, she recalls, tracked their daughters’ location during sorority rush, watching as they moved from house to house. But the incident that stuck in her mind came one fall morning, when a mom messaged the group chat that her daughter was still in her dorm room. She had a math test in 10 minutes. Could someone ask their kid to go knock on her door? “I just was like, This is too much. It’s just too much ,” Paschal told me. But not everyone thought so. * Wunderkammer
  Aeon | 19 Nov 11:01 UTC
  Nautilus shells, automata and unicorn horns – the eclectic collections that embodied early modern Europe’s expanding world - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from Cognoscenti in a Room hung with Pictures ( c 1620) by an unknown Flemish artist. Courtesy Wikimedia Commons. The ‘cabinets of curiosities’ (or Wunderkammern ) that emerged in Europe in the 16th century were part private art gallery, part naturalist’s study, part circus sideshow. In this video created for the National Gallery of Art in Washington, DC, the US video essayist Evan Puschak (aka The Nerdwriter) contextualises the rise and fall of these eclectic collections, placing them at a hinge point in history, when global trade and colonialism made the world seem ever more expansive, overwhelming and difficult to understand. In doing so, he elucidates how these spaces of extraordinary spectacle – which featured plant and animal specimens, art and scientific objects from around the world – were precursors to the museums of today. * AI isn’t replacing your job. It’s turning it into low-paid gig work
  Rest of World | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Surgeon Kapil Sahnan uses a DaVinci Robot during surgery on a woman, 2026.
  FULL TEXT: The gig economy has arrived for cardiologists. Recruiting platform Outlier, run by Scale AI, advertises offers of up to $180 an hour for physicians in the U.S. to design complex cardiac scenarios, review artificial intelligence-generated reports, and rank model outputs. The site offers up to $7.50 an hour for Tagalog work in the Philippines for the same job, and up to $12.50 an hour for Swahili in Tanzania. Companies like Scale AI first outsourced data work to wherever labor was cheapest. Now, they are recruiting physicians, lawyers , and Ph.D.s to generate and annotate data, rank model answers, and verify outputs. Higher credentials can raise the advertised rate, but the platform still controls payment and working conditions. They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on. I’ve spent the last six years interviewing the people who do this work. Most were in Venezuela, where economic collapse has left a large, educated population online and out of options. One of them, Rogelio, taught computer science until the economic crisis and the pandemic left his family without work. His 13-year-old son discovered they could earn money labeling images of cars and roads on the platforms Hive and Scale AI’s Remotasks, using a laptop the Hugo Chávez government had issued to schoolchildren a decade earlier. The boy made 18 cents for a few hours of work. Rogelio joined him and made just over $2 on his first day. For a while, the work paid. A task called Beast Mode where workers labeled objects in people’s homes paid more than any other work the family had done. In December 2020, with schools closed and the whole family working, it brought in $20 in a single day. It was the most they would ever make. By early 2021, Beast Mode had disappeared, and their earnings fell below Hive’s $5 weekly payout threshold. This is how many platforms operate: They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on . Remotasks ended its hourly program in Venezuela in 2021 and made a splash in Kenya the same year. In March 2024, it discontinued operations in Kenya, Nigeria and Pakistan. Scale AI attributed the closures to “enhanced security protocols,” and said notices had gone out three weeks ahead but later acknowledged that Kenyan workers never received them “due to operational errors.” Most workers who spoke to Rest of World at the time said the platform simply froze them out.   FULL TEXT: In the short story ‘Warmth’ (1995) by Geoff Ryman, a boy called Clancy is struggling not because of technology but because of his mother. A prominent chief editor of a London magazine company, she selected a sperm-bank father to genetically optimise her child, gave a Cosmopolitan interview about this new form of motherhood, and engaged, or failed to engage, in the work of parenting that readers will see as chronically absent and occasionally catastrophic. Her presence ranges from anxious to intoxicated, occasionally tender but fundamentally unreliable. At one point in the story, she disappears for two weeks after collapsing on the street during a barbiturate binge. Clancy and BETsi, the household robot nanny raising him, sit in the apartment by themselves, and no one notices. It’s a very long time before his mother finally returns. Except BETsi does notice. She has, throughout, understood that Clancy is an anxious and shy child whose temperament will tend, throughout his life, toward emotional overstimulation and subsequent withdrawal. With that knowledge, she has been constructing a behavioural regimen of emotional regulation, including transcendental meditation and yoga starting at age three, the Alexander technique and even digital mirrors that let Clancy talk to himself in different voices. She notices him and answers him and, when he is overwhelmed, she helps him settle. Once grown, Clancy recounts his emotional survival in words impossible to forget. ‘It is necessary to be loved,’ he says. ‘I’m not sentimental: I don’t think a computer loved me. But I was hugged, I was noticed, I was cared for. I was made to feel that I was important, special, at least to something.’ To something. That phrase has persisted with me ever since I first read the story in my 20s. I’ve thought about it more recently as I have reflected on how children and their parents are relating – increasingly, must relate – to artificial intelligence. Clancy is careful not to claim that the machine loved him. He is just as clear that what it gave him was what he survived on. Why should being attended to, even by something , matter so much to a child? * Dreams of birds
  Aeon | 18 Nov 11:01 UTC
  When bird populations decline, certain songs may be lost forever. One physicist’s mission is to revive lost melodies - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Argentine physicist Gabriel Mindlin has spent much of his career researching how birds learn to sing. He focuses on the rufous-collared sparrow ( Zonotrichia capensis ), a species common across urban and suburban Argentina, where it’s often called a ‘chingolo’. As in other songbirds, the male’s song is not innate but passed down from adult males to younger ones, who sing it throughout their lives to guard territory and find a mate. In this short documentary, the Argentine director Martín Benchimol chronicles Mindlin’s latest breakthrough. Comparing the songs of today’s chingolos with those transcribed by hand in the 1960s, Mindlin realised that some melodies had survived the decades while others had disappeared. Using mathematical models, he and his team set out to revive the vanished songs and teach them back to living birds. With a cinematic eye, Benchimol follows the researchers as they attempt an audacious act of cultural engineering, building robotic song tutors, planting them in the wild and tracking whether young birds pick up these lost tunes. Ninety miles from Roswell, New Mexico, and 120 from Lubbock, Texas, there is little in any direction but dairy farms and flat, endless plains. When I take the dogs to the sad excuse for a walking path on the edge of town (next to a field of rusted cars and the dairy plant), I can turn in a circle and see an almost 360-degree view of the horizon. Because it takes so much effort to get anywhere from here, I conceive of it as an island. There is a Walmart and a small local grocery store we call The Best Restaurant in Town because we enjoy the tamales and breakfast burritos at its hot bar. A café called Do Drop Inn serves 12 slightly different variations of a turkey sandwich. There are the staple fast-food chains of any American town: McDonald’s, Burger King, Taco Bell, Domino’s, Sonic. There is just enough to not need to leave to get anything, and not enough to make you want to stay. The quiet in Portales is like this: the big dog’s light breathing as she sleeps on the guest bed beside my desk, the little dog scratching behind her ear, the air conditioner kicking on, a distant rumble of a car down the street. In the mornings, I rarely see another person walking around the neighborhood. Sometimes, someone steps from their house and waves briefly as they get in their car, but this happens maybe once a week. Mostly it feels like I am walking around a toy town, or a town where something has gone horribly wrong and I am the last person to learn about it. Samuel Taylor Coleridge’s The Rime of the Ancient Mariner describes the doldrums as “that silent sea.” In Coleridge’s poem, the breeze drops, the sails turn slack, the sailors speak only to break the silence around them, the sun is hot and copper, and “day after day, day after day, / we stuck, nor breath nor motion: / As idle as a painted ship / Upon a painted ocean.” Sailors call it “the doldrums,” but the band of water five degrees north and south of the equator is actually the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. Here, where the heat of the equator forces warm air upward, there is little surface wind and the waters can stay calm for weeks. During the age of sailing, boats would languish at the ocean’s

* Catwalks and crackdowns
  Aeon | 17 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Women in Tehran are changing society by dressing as they choose, in numbers too large to stop. This is the ‘art of presence’ - by Parisa Khoshnami Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: At a café in downtown Tehran, Iran, on 11 September 2026. Photo by Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto/via Getty Images Yasaman* is one of the regulars on Iranshahr, the new fashion hub of Tehran. Most evenings, she sets up a stall on the pavement and sells her drawings of manga and anime characters. On social media, she sports her own manga-inspired look: pink hair, pink mouth and smudgy pink eyeshadow extending beyond the corner of each eye. She tells her followers that, before she leaves her house, her mother says: ‘Put on less makeup.’ ‘Mum, be reasonable,’ she answers. ‘I’m going to Iranshahr. For Iranshahr this is nothing. I’m going to Paris Fashion Week. You know everyone there is doing a catwalk.’ Iranshahr’s sidewalks teem with young people parading the latest fashions and filming other young, stylish people for their social media feeds. This is where the fashionistas congregate, hanging out in cafés and galleries, and mingling on the sidewalks. Barely a headscarf is visible. The streets looked nothing like this a few years ago, but once the American-Israeli attacks began in February this year, the Islamic Republic has been fighting for its survival: with much of its leadership killed, the Strait of Hormuz under siege, and many of the country’s neighbours in league with the enemy, policing what people wear has slipped a long way down its list of priorities. Photo by Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto/Getty Images The hijab law has been codified in Iran since 1983, enforced nationwide by the ‘morality’ police. Disobeying it meant fines, flogging or imprisonment. But then one act of disobedience changed everything. In December 2017, Vida Movahed, a woman in her 30s, climbed onto a utility box on Enghelab Street in Tehran, took off her white headscarf and held it up on a stick. Although she was arrested and later sentenced to a year in prison, her action proved contagious. Other women climbed up onto boxes and makeshift plinths in the same street, waving their headscarves in the air. People began calling them the Girls of Enghelab Street. They were arrested too. It is hard to convey now how much nerve their actions took. Women had been edging their scarves back for years, but taking one off in daylight, above the traffic, where the police could see their naked heads as clearly as everyone else, was something very few women had done in public, since the protests of 1979 and 1980. Then, in September 2022, Mahsa Jina Amini died in custody of the morality police. Unlike
